Almost too real, if that makes any sense. \n\nLike I was saying, I can hear the diesel engines, I can smell the hot asphalt—and let me tell you, when you are standing next to a fresh lay of asphalt in the middle of July, the heat radiating off that stuff is so intense it practically singes your eyelashes. You don't imagine that kind of heat. You don't dream the smell of tar and exhaust. It's all very, very real. The cars, the people in them... I can see the expressions on their faces. I can see the guy in the silver Honda tapping his steering wheel because he's late for work, or the woman in the SUV trying to hand a juice box to her kid in the backseat while she waits for me to turn the sign. It's all happening right there in front of me, in sharp focus. \n\nIt's never like a movie, either. In a movie, there's a script, right? There's a plot, and you know what's supposed to happen next. But when my mind goes blank like that, there is no script. That's the whole problem. I'm standing there in this incredibly real, loud, dangerous environment, and I'm the one who's supposed to be directing the action, but the instructions in my head are just... gone. So no, the world doesn't feel fake or distant or like a dream. The world is right there, heavy and loud and demanding my attention. It's just my brain that stops participating in it. I'm just rooted to the spot, fully aware of this very real world, just completely unable to interact with it the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any changes in how you perceive yourself or the world around you that are different from these blank spells—like feeling as if you're not really in control of your body, or that the world feels somehow distant or distorted?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt like I wasn't in control of my body. It's really just those blank spells, like I described. I mean, my body does exactly what I tell it to do, it's just that sometimes... I don't have anything to tell it, you know? But I'm always the one driving the car, so to speak. Nobody else is grabbing the steering wheel. I've never felt like a puppet on a string or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for the world feeling distant or distorted... no. Like I was saying about the heat off the asphalt and the smell of the diesel, everything is always right up in my face. Almost aggressively so. There's no distance. If anything, I wish there was a little bit of distance sometimes, especially when the noise from the jackhammers gets going and the dust is blowing everywhere and you've got drivers honking because they think their time is more important than the safety of the crew. But no, everything is always very sharp, very present, very real. \n\nI've never had any weird visual things, either. Like, the stop sign always looks exactly like a stop sign. The cars always look like cars. The trees don't look weird, the sky doesn't look weird. Everything is exactly where it's supposed to be, exactly how it's supposed to look. It's just my brain that sort of... unplugs from it all. But the world itself? And my connection to my own body? That all stays perfectly normal, perfectly solid. It's just the thoughts that vanish. So, yeah, none of that other stuff you mentioned. Just the blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of memory loss or feeling disconnected from your thoughts or surroundings outside of these blank spells while you're working?\n\nPatient: No, no memory loss. And I don't feel disconnected outside of work either. I mean, my memory is actually pretty sharp, all things considered. I can remember the license plate of that guy in the silver Honda I mentioned earlier, for example—it's one of those custom state park plates, the ones with the little blue heron on it, and it ends in 4-G-T. I remember that because I always think, \"Four Grand Touring,\" like the car, even though it's a Honda Civic. So my memory is fine. \n\nAnd as for feeling disconnected when I'm not on the job... no. When I'm at home, I'm just at home. I mean, I'm tired, obviously. You work a ten or twelve-hour shift on your feet, holding a sign that catches the wind like a sail, you're going to be exhausted. I get home, I take off those boots—the ones I really need the gel inserts for—and I just kind of collapse on the couch. But I know I'm on the couch. I know I'm watching Jeopardy, or whatever happens to be on. I know I need to get up and put a frozen pizza in the oven, even if I don't have the energy to actually do it. \n\nI'm very aware of my surroundings, my apartment, the sound of the neighbor upstairs who apparently bowls with medicine balls every Tuesday night. I don't lose time. I don't suddenly \"wake up\" and wonder how I got to the kitchen. I don't forget conversations I've had with my sister when she calls to check in, even if I don't really feel like talking to her because I'm so drained. I remember everything we talked about—how her kids are doing in soccer, how her husband's promotion is going. \n\nIt's really, truly just those specific moments out there on the asphalt when the thoughts just... stop. It's not a memory thing, because once the spell passes, I know exactly what just happened. I know I just stood there like a statue for two minutes. I remember the panic of it. I remember the cars waiting. I just don't have any thoughts *during* it. But outside of that? No, my memory is completely intact, and I'm always fully connected to whatever boring, exhausting thing I happen to be doing at the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not at all. Like I said before, it's never like that. A foggy window... I mean, honestly, sometimes I wish I had a foggy window to look through, just to soften things up a bit, you know? Give my eyes a break. But no. 

Even when I'm not on the site, even when I'm just on my days off or running errands... everything is crystal clear. I'll give you an example. I go to the grocery store, right? The big one down on Elm Street, where the fluorescent lights are so bright they practically hum. I'm walking down the cereal aisle, and there is absolutely no fog. I see every single brightly colored box, I see the price tags—which, by the way, are absolutely ridiculous right now. I mean, almost seven dollars for a box of generic bran flakes? When I was getting my bachelor's degree—which feels like a lifetime ago, honestly, and believe me, I spend a lot of time wondering how I ended up standing on asphalt holding a stop sign after four years of university—but back then, you could feed yourself for a week on seven dollars. 

But anyway, my point is, I'm standing there in the aisle, and I can hear the squeak of a shopping cart wheel from the lady three aisles over. I can see the little smudges on the glass of the freezer doors where somebody's toddler pressed their sticky hands. I can feel the cold air blasting out of the dairy section right onto my ankles. It's all just... aggressively real. 

There's no detachment at all. There's no feeling like it's a movie or a dream or like I'm separated from it by glass. I'm just a forty-one-year-old woman standing in a freezing grocery store staring at overpriced bran flakes, feeling the ache in my lower back from standing on the road all week. It's mundane, it's exhausting, and it is one hundred percent real life. I never get that disconnected feeling you're talking about, not at work, and definitely not at home or anywhere else. It's just those blank spells I told you about. That is literally the only thing. The rest of the time, the window is perfectly, annoyingly clean.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because something is just fundamentally wrong with my head lately, and it's starting to really scare me, especially with my job. I'm a crossing guard and a flagger, you know, for the construction sites and the school zones, and I've been doing this for years, I have a bachelor's degree, I'm a smart woman, but lately... it's like my mind just completely empties out. It goes entirely blank at the absolute worst possible times. Just the other day, I was standing out there with the stop-slow paddle, the same one I've held thousands of times, and I just stared at it. I couldn't remember which side to turn to the traffic. Or I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, the standard ones we have to fill out every single morning before the crew even gets there, and the words just look like... like shapes. I can't process them. I can't remember the steps, the basic safety protocols I could normally do in my sleep. It's not just forgetting a name or where I put my keys, it's this heavy, thick fog that rolls in and just erases my ability to think or process anything right when I need it most, and with cars speeding by, I just can't afford to have my brain shut off like that, you know? It's terrifying.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any particular event or situation that seems to trigger these episodes, or does it seem to happen out of the blue?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, doctor, I have racked my brain trying to figure that out. I really have. Because if there was a trigger, you know, if it was just when it rains, or just when I'm dealing with an angry driver—and believe me, people get so incredibly angry when you make them wait thirty seconds for a dump truck to back out, they roll down their windows and yell the most awful things at you—but it's not that. It's not stress from the job. It's completely, entirely out of the blue. \n\nI could be having a perfectly fine morning, I've had my coffee, I've listened to the local news on the radio, the weather is clear, I'm standing at my post at 8:00 AM feeling completely normal, and then... bam. Nothing. The fog just drops down on me. But then, another time, it'll happen at 2:30 in the afternoon on a Tuesday when the construction site is actually quiet and I'm just waiting for the school buses to start their afternoon routes. \n\nI've tried keeping a little mental log, you know? Like, did I eat enough breakfast? Did I sleep wrong? Was it the noise level from the jackhammers? Because I have my degree, I know how to analyze things, I know how to look for variables and patterns, but there is absolutely no rhyme or reason to it that I can find. It just sneaks up on me, completely unprovoked. I'll just be standing there, and suddenly my brain is just gone. And I think that's what makes it so much more terrifying, the not knowing when it's going to strike next. If I knew what caused it, I could avoid it, but since I can't... I just never know when I'm going to lose my mind out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other symptoms happening along with these episodes, like headaches, dizziness, vision changes, or any unusual movements?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, I really try to pay attention to my body, you know? Being out on your feet all day, you have to. You have to know if you're getting dehydrated or if the sun is getting to you, especially in the summer when the asphalt is just radiating heat and you're wearing that heavy high-visibility gear. But no, my vision is perfectly fine, I don't get dizzy... well, maybe just the normal kind of dizzy if you stand up too fast, but not when these... these blank spells happen. \n\nAnd headaches, no, not really. I mean, I get the occasional tension headache, who doesn't? But it's not connected to the fog. It's not like an aura or a warning sign or anything. I don't get a pounding in my temples right before my brain shuts off. And movements? No, my hands don't shake, I don't twitch or anything like that. I'm just... still. I'm just standing there, physically perfectly fine, holding the sign, wearing the vest, but mentally I'm just... gone. It's purely in my head, doctor. Just this heavy, empty nothingness. Physically, I feel exactly the same as I did ten minutes before it happened. It's so frustrating because if I had a headache or if I felt dizzy, at least I could tell my supervisor, \"Hey, I'm feeling sick, I need to sit in the truck for a minute.\" But I don't feel sick. I just feel... blank.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank spells ever happen when you're asleep or just when you're awake?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, they only happen when I'm awake. Because, you know, when I'm asleep, I'm asleep. I've always been a pretty solid sleeper, actually, even with the weird hours sometimes required for construction jobs, like those night paving crews where you're out there at 2:00 AM under those massive floodlights. But no, these spells, this terrifying blankness, it's strictly a waking nightmare. \n\nLike I was saying earlier, and I know I'm probably repeating myself, but it's just so consuming—it happens right in the middle of my day, right when I'm awake and trying to function. I'll be wide awake, looking down at those standard road-closure forms that I've filled out a million times since I started this job, or I'll be gripping that stop-slow paddle, and suddenly, boom. The thoughts just evaporate. I'm awake, my eyes are open, but my mind just keeps going completely blank at the absolute worst possible times. I'm standing there, fully conscious, and I just cannot remember the basic steps I have done for years. It's not a dream, it's not sleepwalking. I'm awake, and my brain just decides to pull the plug on my ability to think.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your mind goes blank, do you lose awareness of what’s happening around you, or do you remain aware but unable to think clearly?\n\nPatient: I am completely aware, and honestly, doctor, I think that is the absolute worst part of the entire experience. I am fully, painfully aware of every single thing happening around me. I don't black out, the world doesn't go dark, I don't lose time. I can see the cars lined up, I can hear the diesel engines idling, I can feel the gravel crunching under my boots, and I can see the drivers staring at me through their windshields, waiting for me to do something. \n\nBut I just... I can't think. I cannot form a single coherent thought. It's exactly why I finally came in to get help, because my mind just keeps going completely blank at the absolute worst possible times. Like I was saying before about the stop-slow paddle and those road-closure forms—I'll be looking right at them, fully conscious, totally awake, but I suddenly cannot remember the steps I have done for years. It's like the filing cabinet in my brain has just been locked and somebody threw away the key. \n\nI'll be staring at the letters on the sign, S-T-O-P, and I know it's a word, I know I'm at work, but the connection between seeing the sign and knowing what action I am supposed to take next is just severed. Do I flip it? Do I wave the lead car through? Do I radio my partner at the other end of the work zone? I don't know. The knowledge is just gone. So I'm just standing there, trapped inside my own head, watching the traffic and listening to the construction noise, completely aware that I am failing to do my job, but entirely unable to make my brain process the information. It's like being a prisoner in your own body while the rest of the world just keeps moving at normal speed around you.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a moment during these episodes where you felt like you were watching yourself from outside your body or felt detached from who you really are?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I've heard of people having those kinds of out-of-body experiences, like on TV or whatever, where they say they float up to the ceiling or the sky and look down at themselves. But no, for me, it's honestly the exact opposite. I am so incredibly trapped inside my body when it happens. \n\nI feel every ounce of that heavy, sweaty safety vest. I feel the exact way my steel-toed boots are pinching my pinky toe on the right foot—because I've been meaning to get new gel inserts for those boots for about three months now, but I just haven't had the energy to go to the workwear store after a ten-hour shift on the asphalt. Anyway, I am definitely not floating away. I'm right there. I'm intensely aware of being me, being in my body, gripping that fiberglass pole. It's just that the 'me' inside the body suddenly doesn't have any thoughts to work with. \n\nI don't feel detached from who I really am, it's more like... who I am is just sitting in a dark, empty room waiting for the lights to come back on. I'm fully in my own skin, doctor, I promise you that. It's just a very empty skin when those spells hit. And it's so incredibly frustrating, you know? Because if I could just float away for a minute, if I could just detach, maybe it wouldn't be so completely terrifying to stand there staring at a line of impatient commuters in F-150s and massive commercial dump trucks, knowing I am the only thing keeping them from crashing into my paving crew, but having absolutely zero idea how to signal them. So, no. No floating, no watching myself from the outside. Just being stuck right there in the middle of the road, perfectly inside my own body, but completely, terrifyingly empty.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like the world around you isn't real, or like you're living in a dream or a movie?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like a dream at all. I mean, I know what you're asking, like that feeling when you wake up and for a split second you aren't sure if you're awake or still dreaming? It's not like that. The world feels completely, entirely real. Almost too real, if that makes any sense. \n\nLike I was saying, I can hear the diesel engines, I can smell the hot asphalt—and let me tell you, when you are standing next to a fresh lay of asphalt in the middle of July, the heat radiating off that stuff is so intense it practically singes your eyelashes. You don't imagine that kind of heat. You don't dream the smell of tar and exhaust. It's all very, very real. The cars, the people in them... I can see the expressions on their faces. I can see the guy in the silver Honda tapping his steering wheel because he's late for work, or the woman in the SUV trying to hand a juice box to her kid in the backseat while she waits for me to turn the sign. It's all happening right there in front of me, in sharp focus. \n\nIt's never like a movie, either. In a movie, there's a script, right? There's a plot, and you know what's supposed to happen next. But when my mind goes blank like that, there is no script. That's the whole problem. I'm standing there in this incredibly real, loud, dangerous environment, and I'm the one who's supposed to be directing the action, but the instructions in my head are just... gone. So no, the world doesn't feel fake or distant or like a dream. The world is right there, heavy and loud and demanding my attention. It's just my brain that stops participating in it. I'm just rooted to the spot, fully aware of this very real world, just completely unable to interact with it the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any changes in how you perceive yourself or the world around you that are different from these blank spells—like feeling as if you're not really in control of your body, or that the world feels somehow distant or distorted?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt like I wasn't in control of my body. It's really just those blank spells, like I described. I mean, my body does exactly what I tell it to do, it's just that sometimes... I don't have anything to tell it, you know? But I'm always the one driving the car, so to speak. Nobody else is grabbing the steering wheel. I've never felt like a puppet on a string or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for the world feeling distant or distorted... no. Like I was saying about the heat off the asphalt and the smell of the diesel, everything is always right up in my face. Almost aggressively so. There's no distance. If anything, I wish there was a little bit of distance sometimes, especially when the noise from the jackhammers gets going and the dust is blowing everywhere and you've got drivers honking because they think their time is more important than the safety of the crew. But no, everything is always very sharp, very present, very real. \n\nI've never had any weird visual things, either. Like, the stop sign always looks exactly like a stop sign. The cars always look like cars. The trees don't look weird, the sky doesn't look weird. Everything is exactly where it's supposed to be, exactly how it's supposed to look. It's just my brain that sort of... unplugs from it all. But the world itself? And my connection to my own body? That all stays perfectly normal, perfectly solid. It's just the thoughts that vanish. So, yeah, none of that other stuff you mentioned. Just the blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of memory loss or feeling disconnected from your thoughts or surroundings outside of these blank spells while you're working?\n\nPatient: No, no memory loss. And I don't feel disconnected outside of work either. I mean, my memory is actually pretty sharp, all things considered. I can remember the license plate of that guy in the silver Honda I mentioned earlier, for example—it's one of those custom state park plates, the ones with the little blue heron on it, and it ends in 4-G-T. I remember that because I always think, \"Four Grand Touring,\" like the car, even though it's a Honda Civic. So my memory is fine. \n\nAnd as for feeling disconnected when I'm not on the job... no. When I'm at home, I'm just at home. I mean, I'm tired, obviously. You work a ten or twelve-hour shift on your feet, holding a sign that catches the wind like a sail, you're going to be exhausted. I get home, I take off those boots—the ones I really need the gel inserts for—and I just kind of collapse on the couch. But I know I'm on the couch. I know I'm watching Jeopardy, or whatever happens to be on. I know I need to get up and put a frozen pizza in the oven, even if I don't have the energy to actually do it. \n\nI'm very aware of my surroundings, my apartment, the sound of the neighbor upstairs who apparently bowls with medicine balls every Tuesday night. I don't lose time. I don't suddenly \"wake up\" and wonder how I got to the kitchen. I don't forget conversations I've had with my sister when she calls to check in, even if I don't really feel like talking to her because I'm so drained. I remember everything we talked about—how her kids are doing in soccer, how her husband's promotion is going. \n\nIt's really, truly just those specific moments out there on the asphalt when the thoughts just... stop. It's not a memory thing, because once the spell passes, I know exactly what just happened. I know I just stood there like a statue for two minutes. I remember the panic of it. I remember the cars waiting. I just don't have any thoughts *during* it. But outside of that? No, my memory is completely intact, and I'm always fully connected to whatever boring, exhausting thing I happen to be doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or like you were watching life through a foggy window, even when you weren’t having one of these blank episodes at work?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, it's never like that. A foggy window... I mean, honestly, sometimes I wish I had a foggy window to look through, just to soften things up a bit, you know? Give my eyes a break. But no. \n\nEven when I'm not on the site, even when I'm just on my days off or running errands... everything is crystal clear. I'll give you an example. I go to the grocery store, right? The big one down on Elm Street, where the fluorescent lights are so bright they practically hum. I'm walking down the cereal aisle, and there is absolutely no fog. I see every single brightly colored box, I see the price tags—which, by the way, are absolutely ridiculous right now. I mean, almost seven dollars for a box of generic bran flakes? When I was getting my bachelor's degree—which feels like a lifetime ago, honestly, and believe me, I spend a lot of time wondering how I ended up standing on asphalt holding a stop sign after four years of university—but back then, you could feed yourself for a week on seven dollars. \n\nBut anyway, my point is, I'm standing there in the aisle, and I can hear the squeak of a shopping cart wheel from the lady three aisles over. I can see the little smudges on the glass of the freezer doors where somebody's toddler pressed their sticky hands. I can feel the cold air blasting out of the dairy section right onto my ankles. It's all just... aggressively real. \n\nThere's no detachment at all. There's no feeling like it's a movie or a dream or like I'm separated from it by glass. I'm just a forty-one-year-old woman standing in a freezing grocery store staring at overpriced bran flakes, feeling the ache in my lower back from standing on the road all week. It's mundane, it's exhausting, and it is one hundred percent real life. I never get that disconnected feeling you're talking about, not at work, and definitely not at home or anywhere else. It's just those blank spells I told you about. That is literally the only thing. The rest of the time, the window is perfectly, annoyingly clean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I can remember the license plate of that guy in the silver Honda I mentioned earlier, for example—it's one of those custom state park plates, the ones with the little blue heron on it, and it ends in 4-G-T. I remember that because I always think, \"Four Grand Touring,\" like the car, even though it's a Honda Civic. So my memory is fine. \n\nAnd as for feeling disconnected when I'm not on the job... no. When I'm at home, I'm just at home. I mean, I'm tired, obviously. You work a ten or twelve-hour shift on your feet, holding a sign that catches the wind like a sail, you're going to be exhausted. I get home, I take off those boots—the ones I really need the gel inserts for—and I just kind of collapse on the couch. But I know I'm on the couch. I know I'm watching Jeopardy, or whatever happens to be on. I know I need to get up and put a frozen pizza in the oven, even if I don't have the energy to actually do it. \n\nI'm very aware of my surroundings, my apartment, the sound of the neighbor upstairs who apparently bowls with medicine balls every Tuesday night. I don't lose time. I don't suddenly \"wake up\" and wonder how I got to the kitchen. I don't forget conversations I've had with my sister when she calls to check in, even if I don't really feel like talking to her because I'm so drained. I remember everything we talked about—how her kids are doing in soccer, how her husband's promotion is going. \n\nIt's really, truly just those specific moments out there on the asphalt when the thoughts just... stop. It's not a memory thing, because once the spell passes, I know exactly what just happened. I know I just stood there like a statue for two minutes. I remember the panic of it. I remember the cars waiting. I just don't have any thoughts *during* it. But outside of that? No, my memory is completely intact, and I'm always fully connected to whatever boring, exhausting thing I happen to be doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or like you were watching life through a foggy window, even when you weren’t having one of these blank episodes at work?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, it's never like that. A foggy window... I mean, honestly, sometimes I wish I had a foggy window to look through, just to soften things up a bit, you know? Give my eyes a break. But no. \n\nEven when I'm not on the site, even when I'm just on my days off or running errands... everything is crystal clear. I'll give you an example. I go to the grocery store, right? The big one down on Elm Street, where the fluorescent lights are so bright they practically hum. I'm walking down the cereal aisle, and there is absolutely no fog. I see every single brightly colored box, I see the price tags—which, by the way, are absolutely ridiculous right now. I mean, almost seven dollars for a box of generic bran flakes? When I was getting my bachelor's degree—which feels like a lifetime ago, honestly, and believe me, I spend a lot of time wondering how I ended up standing on asphalt holding a stop sign after four years of university—but back then, you could feed yourself for a week on seven dollars. \n\nBut anyway, my point is, I'm standing there in the aisle, and I can hear the squeak of a shopping cart wheel from the lady three aisles over. I can see the little smudges on the glass of the freezer doors where somebody's toddler pressed their sticky hands. I can feel the cold air blasting out of the dairy section right onto my ankles. It's all just... aggressively real. \n\nThere's no detachment at all. There's no feeling like it's a movie or a dream or like I'm separated from it by glass. I'm just a forty-one-year-old woman standing in a freezing grocery store staring at overpriced bran flakes, feeling the ache in my lower back from standing on the road all week. It's mundane, it's exhausting, and it is one hundred percent real life. I never get that disconnected feeling you're talking about, not at work, and definitely not at home or anywhere else. It's just those blank spells I told you about. That is literally the only thing. The rest of the time, the window is perfectly, annoyingly clean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, never. I've never wandered off or found myself somewhere without knowing how I got there. Like I was saying about my memory being sharp, I always know exactly what I'm doing and why I'm doing it. Even when I'm doing things on autopilot, you know? 

Like, take my morning routine. I get up at 4:30 AM, which is ungodly, but you have to be at the site early before the rush hour traffic starts getting really aggressive. So I get up, I make my coffee—just instant, I don't have the patience or the energy for a French press or anything fancy at that hour, not that I could afford the good beans anyway. I put on my high-vis vest, I lace up those boots with the gel inserts, I grab my thermos, and I drive to the site. It's the same routine every single day, five days a week, sometimes six if they need me for a weekend closure. I could do it in my sleep. 

But I'm not *actually* asleep when I do it. I'm fully aware. I know I'm making the instant coffee. I know I'm lacing up the boots. I don't ever snap out of a daze and think, "Wait, how did I get my boots on?" or "Why am I holding my car keys?" I know exactly why I'm holding them. Because if I don't go to work, I don't get paid, and if I don't get paid, I can't pay the rent on my apartment, which they just raised by another hundred dollars a month, by the way. 

So, no, there's no wandering, no confusion, no doing things without realizing it. Even when I'm exhausted, even when my brain feels like it's been put through a blender from the noise and the heat and the stress of trying to keep people from driving their cars into a ditch, I am always aware of what I'm doing. It's just those blank spells, right there on the job, where everything just stops. But I'm never doing anything during those spells. I'm just standing there. I'm not wandering into traffic or twirling my sign around without knowing it. I'm just frozen. So, no, none of that confused wandering stuff. Not at all.

Just the other day, I was standing out there with the stop-slow paddle, the same one I've held thousands of times, and I just stared at it. I couldn't remember which side to turn to the traffic. Or I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, the standard ones we have to fill out every single morning before the crew even gets there, and the words just look like... like shapes. I can't process them. I can't remember the steps, the basic safety protocols I could normally do in my sleep. It's not just forgetting a name or where I put my keys, it's this heavy, thick fog that rolls in and just erases my ability to think or process anything right when I need it most, and with cars speeding by, I just can't afford to have my brain shut off like that, you know? It's terrifying.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any particular event or situation that seems to trigger these episodes, or does it seem to happen out of the blue?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, doctor, I have racked my brain trying to figure that out. I really have. Because if there was a trigger, you know, if it was just when it rains, or just when I'm dealing with an angry driver—and believe me, people get so incredibly angry when you make them wait thirty seconds for a dump truck to back out, they roll down their windows and yell the most awful things at you—but it's not that. It's not stress from the job. It's completely, entirely out of the blue. \n\nI could be having a perfectly fine morning, I've had my coffee, I've listened to the local news on the radio, the weather is clear, I'm standing at my post at 8:00 AM feeling completely normal, and then... bam. Nothing. The fog just drops down on me. But then, another time, it'll happen at 2:30 in the afternoon on a Tuesday when the construction site is actually quiet and I'm just waiting for the school buses to start their afternoon routes. \n\nI've tried keeping a little mental log, you know? Like, did I eat enough breakfast? Did I sleep wrong? Was it the noise level from the jackhammers? Because I have my degree, I know how to analyze things, I know how to look for variables and patterns, but there is absolutely no rhyme or reason to it that I can find. It just sneaks up on me, completely unprovoked. I'll just be standing there, and suddenly my brain is just gone. And I think that's what makes it so much more terrifying, the not knowing when it's going to strike next. If I knew what caused it, I could avoid it, but since I can't... I just never know when I'm going to lose my mind out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other symptoms happening along with these episodes, like headaches, dizziness, vision changes, or any unusual movements?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, I really try to pay attention to my body, you know? Being out on your feet all day, you have to. You have to know if you're getting dehydrated or if the sun is getting to you, especially in the summer when the asphalt is just radiating heat and you're wearing that heavy high-visibility gear. But no, my vision is perfectly fine, I don't get dizzy... well, maybe just the normal kind of dizzy if you stand up too fast, but not when these... these blank spells happen. \n\nAnd headaches, no, not really. I mean, I get the occasional tension headache, who doesn't? But it's not connected to the fog. It's not like an aura or a warning sign or anything. I don't get a pounding in my temples right before my brain shuts off. And movements? No, my hands don't shake, I don't twitch or anything like that. I'm just... still. I'm just standing there, physically perfectly fine, holding the sign, wearing the vest, but mentally I'm just... gone. It's purely in my head, doctor. Just this heavy, empty nothingness. Physically, I feel exactly the same as I did ten minutes before it happened. It's so frustrating because if I had a headache or if I felt dizzy, at least I could tell my supervisor, \"Hey, I'm feeling sick, I need to sit in the truck for a minute.\" But I don't feel sick. I just feel... blank.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank spells ever happen when you're asleep or just when you're awake?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, they only happen when I'm awake. Because, you know, when I'm asleep, I'm asleep. I've always been a pretty solid sleeper, actually, even with the weird hours sometimes required for construction jobs, like those night paving crews where you're out there at 2:00 AM under those massive floodlights. But no, these spells, this terrifying blankness, it's strictly a waking nightmare. \n\nLike I was saying earlier, and I know I'm probably repeating myself, but it's just so consuming—it happens right in the middle of my day, right when I'm awake and trying to function. I'll be wide awake, looking down at those standard road-closure forms that I've filled out a million times since I started this job, or I'll be gripping that stop-slow paddle, and suddenly, boom. The thoughts just evaporate. I'm awake, my eyes are open, but my mind just keeps going completely blank at the absolute worst possible times. I'm standing there, fully conscious, and I just cannot remember the basic steps I have done for years. It's not a dream, it's not sleepwalking. I'm awake, and my brain just decides to pull the plug on my ability to think.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your mind goes blank, do you lose awareness of what’s happening around you, or do you remain aware but unable to think clearly?\n\nPatient: I am completely aware, and honestly, doctor, I think that is the absolute worst part of the entire experience. I am fully, painfully aware of every single thing happening around me. I don't black out, the world doesn't go dark, I don't lose time. I can see the cars lined up, I can hear the diesel engines idling, I can feel the gravel crunching under my boots, and I can see the drivers staring at me through their windshields, waiting for me to do something. \n\nBut I just... I can't think. I cannot form a single coherent thought. It's exactly why I finally came in to get help, because my mind just keeps going completely blank at the absolute worst possible times. Like I was saying before about the stop-slow paddle and those road-closure forms—I'll be looking right at them, fully conscious, totally awake, but I suddenly cannot remember the steps I have done for years. It's like the filing cabinet in my brain has just been locked and somebody threw away the key. \n\nI'll be staring at the letters on the sign, S-T-O-P, and I know it's a word, I know I'm at work, but the connection between seeing the sign and knowing what action I am supposed to take next is just severed. Do I flip it? Do I wave the lead car through? Do I radio my partner at the other end of the work zone? I don't know. The knowledge is just gone. So I'm just standing there, trapped inside my own head, watching the traffic and listening to the construction noise, completely aware that I am failing to do my job, but entirely unable to make my brain process the information. It's like being a prisoner in your own body while the rest of the world just keeps moving at normal speed around you.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a moment during these episodes where you felt like you were watching yourself from outside your body or felt detached from who you really are?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I've heard of people having those kinds of out-of-body experiences, like on TV or whatever, where they say they float up to the ceiling or the sky and look down at themselves. But no, for me, it's honestly the exact opposite. I am so incredibly trapped inside my body when it happens. \n\nI feel every ounce of that heavy, sweaty safety vest. I feel the exact way my steel-toed boots are pinching my pinky toe on the right foot—because I've been meaning to get new gel inserts for those boots for about three months now, but I just haven't had the energy to go to the workwear store after a ten-hour shift on the asphalt. Anyway, I am definitely not floating away. I'm right there. I'm intensely aware of being me, being in my body, gripping that fiberglass pole. It's just that the 'me' inside the body suddenly doesn't have any thoughts to work with. \n\nI don't feel detached from who I really am, it's more like... who I am is just sitting in a dark, empty room waiting for the lights to come back on. I'm fully in my own skin, doctor, I promise you that. It's just a very empty skin when those spells hit. And it's so incredibly frustrating, you know? Because if I could just float away for a minute, if I could just detach, maybe it wouldn't be so completely terrifying to stand there staring at a line of impatient commuters in F-150s and massive commercial dump trucks, knowing I am the only thing keeping them from crashing into my paving crew, but having absolutely zero idea how to signal them. So, no. No floating, no watching myself from the outside. Just being stuck right there in the middle of the road, perfectly inside my own body, but completely, terrifyingly empty.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like the world around you isn't real, or like you're living in a dream or a movie?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like a dream at all. I mean, I know what you're asking, like that feeling when you wake up and for a split second you aren't sure if you're awake or still dreaming? It's not like that. The world feels completely, entirely real. Almost too real, if that makes any sense. \n\nLike I was saying, I can hear the diesel engines, I can smell the hot asphalt—and let me tell you, when you are standing next to a fresh lay of asphalt in the middle of July, the heat radiating off that stuff is so intense it practically singes your eyelashes. You don't imagine that kind of heat. You don't dream the smell of tar and exhaust. It's all very, very real. The cars, the people in them... I can see the expressions on their faces. I can see the guy in the silver Honda tapping his steering wheel because he's late for work, or the woman in the SUV trying to hand a juice box to her kid in the backseat while she waits for me to turn the sign. It's all happening right there in front of me, in sharp focus. \n\nIt's never like a movie, either. In a movie, there's a script, right? There's a plot, and you know what's supposed to happen next. But when my mind goes blank like that, there is no script. That's the whole problem. I'm standing there in this incredibly real, loud, dangerous environment, and I'm the one who's supposed to be directing the action, but the instructions in my head are just... gone. So no, the world doesn't feel fake or distant or like a dream. The world is right there, heavy and loud and demanding my attention. It's just my brain that stops participating in it. I'm just rooted to the spot, fully aware of this very real world, just completely unable to interact with it the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any changes in how you perceive yourself or the world around you that are different from these blank spells—like feeling as if you're not really in control of your body, or that the world feels somehow distant or distorted?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt like I wasn't in control of my body. It's really just those blank spells, like I described. I mean, my body does exactly what I tell it to do, it's just that sometimes... I don't have anything to tell it, you know? But I'm always the one driving the car, so to speak. Nobody else is grabbing the steering wheel. I've never felt like a puppet on a string or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for the world feeling distant or distorted... no. Like I was saying about the heat off the asphalt and the smell of the diesel, everything is always right up in my face. Almost aggressively so. There's no distance. If anything, I wish there was a little bit of distance sometimes, especially when the noise from the jackhammers gets going and the dust is blowing everywhere and you've got drivers honking because they think their time is more important than the safety of the crew. But no, everything is always very sharp, very present, very real. \n\nI've never had any weird visual things, either. Like, the stop sign always looks exactly like a stop sign. The cars always look like cars. The trees don't look weird, the sky doesn't look weird. Everything is exactly where it's supposed to be, exactly how it's supposed to look. It's just my brain that sort of... unplugs from it all. But the world itself? And my connection to my own body? That all stays perfectly normal, perfectly solid. It's just the thoughts that vanish. So, yeah, none of that other stuff you mentioned. Just the blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of memory loss or feeling disconnected from your thoughts or surroundings outside of these blank spells while you're working?\n\nPatient: No, no memory loss. And I don't feel disconnected outside of work either. I mean, my memory is actually pretty sharp, all things considered. I can remember the license plate of that guy in the silver Honda I mentioned earlier, for example—it's one of those custom state park plates, the ones with the little blue heron on it, and it ends in 4-G-T. I remember that because I always think, \"Four Grand Touring,\" like the car, even though it's a Honda Civic. So my memory is fine. \n\nAnd as for feeling disconnected when I'm not on the job... no. When I'm at home, I'm just at home. I mean, I'm tired, obviously. You work a ten or twelve-hour shift on your feet, holding a sign that catches the wind like a sail, you're going to be exhausted. I get home, I take off those boots—the ones I really need the gel inserts for—and I just kind of collapse on the couch. But I know I'm on the couch. I know I'm watching Jeopardy, or whatever happens to be on. I know I need to get up and put a frozen pizza in the oven, even if I don't have the energy to actually do it. \n\nI'm very aware of my surroundings, my apartment, the sound of the neighbor upstairs who apparently bowls with medicine balls every Tuesday night. I don't lose time. I don't suddenly \"wake up\" and wonder how I got to the kitchen. I don't forget conversations I've had with my sister when she calls to check in, even if I don't really feel like talking to her because I'm so drained. I remember everything we talked about—how her kids are doing in soccer, how her husband's promotion is going. \n\nIt's really, truly just those specific moments out there on the asphalt when the thoughts just... stop. It's not a memory thing, because once the spell passes, I know exactly what just happened. I know I just stood there like a statue for two minutes. I remember the panic of it. I remember the cars waiting. I just don't have any thoughts *during* it. But outside of that? No, my memory is completely intact, and I'm always fully connected to whatever boring, exhausting thing I happen to be doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or like you were watching life through a foggy window, even when you weren’t having one of these blank episodes at work?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, it's never like that. A foggy window... I mean, honestly, sometimes I wish I had a foggy window to look through, just to soften things up a bit, you know? Give my eyes a break. But no. \n\nEven when I'm not on the site, even when I'm just on my days off or running errands... everything is crystal clear. I'll give you an example. I go to the grocery store, right? The big one down on Elm Street, where the fluorescent lights are so bright they practically hum. I'm walking down the cereal aisle, and there is absolutely no fog. I see every single brightly colored box, I see the price tags—which, by the way, are absolutely ridiculous right now. I mean, almost seven dollars for a box of generic bran flakes? When I was getting my bachelor's degree—which feels like a lifetime ago, honestly, and believe me, I spend a lot of time wondering how I ended up standing on asphalt holding a stop sign after four years of university—but back then, you could feed yourself for a week on seven dollars. \n\nBut anyway, my point is, I'm standing there in the aisle, and I can hear the squeak of a shopping cart wheel from the lady three aisles over. I can see the little smudges on the glass of the freezer doors where somebody's toddler pressed their sticky hands. I can feel the cold air blasting out of the dairy section right onto my ankles. It's all just... aggressively real. \n\nThere's no detachment at all. There's no feeling like it's a movie or a dream or like I'm separated from it by glass. I'm just a forty-one-year-old woman standing in a freezing grocery store staring at overpriced bran flakes, feeling the ache in my lower back from standing on the road all week. It's mundane, it's exhausting, and it is one hundred percent real life. I never get that disconnected feeling you're talking about, not at work, and definitely not at home or anywhere else. It's just those blank spells I told you about. That is literally the only thing. The rest of the time, the window is perfectly, annoyingly clean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you suddenly felt dazed, confused, or found yourself doing things without being fully aware of it, like wandering somewhere or performing routine actions without remembering why?\n\nPatient: No, never. I've never wandered off or found myself somewhere without knowing how I got there. Like I was saying about my memory being sharp, I always know exactly what I'm doing and why I'm doing it. Even when I'm doing things on autopilot, you know? \n\nLike, take my morning routine. I get up at 4:30 AM, which is ungodly, but you have to be at the site early before the rush hour traffic starts getting really aggressive. So I get up, I make my coffee—just instant, I don't have the patience or the energy for a French press or anything fancy at that hour, not that I could afford the good beans anyway. I put on my high-vis vest, I lace up those boots with the gel inserts, I grab my thermos, and I drive to the site. It's the same routine every single day, five days a week, sometimes six if they need me for a weekend closure. I could do it in my sleep. \n\nBut I'm not *actually* asleep when I do it. I'm fully aware. I know I'm making the instant coffee. I know I'm lacing up the boots. I don't ever snap out of a daze and think, \"Wait, how did I get my boots on?\" or \"Why am I holding my car keys?\" I know exactly why I'm holding them. Because if I don't go to work, I don't get paid, and if I don't get paid, I can't pay the rent on my apartment, which they just raised by another hundred dollars a month, by the way. \n\nSo, no, there's no wandering, no confusion, no doing things without realizing it. Even when I'm exhausted, even when my brain feels like it's been put through a blender from the noise and the heat and the stress of trying to keep people from driving their cars into a ditch, I am always aware of what I'm doing. It's just those blank spells, right there on the job, where everything just stops. But I'm never doing anything during those spells. I'm just standing there. I'm not wandering into traffic or twirling my sign around without knowing it. I'm just frozen. So, no, none of that confused wandering stuff. Not at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's really just those blank spells, like I described. I mean, my body does exactly what I tell it to do, it's just that sometimes... I don't have anything to tell it, you know? But I'm always the one driving the car, so to speak. Nobody else is grabbing the steering wheel. I've never felt like a puppet on a string or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for the world feeling distant or distorted... no. Like I was saying about the heat off the asphalt and the smell of the diesel, everything is always right up in my face. Almost aggressively so. There's no distance. If anything, I wish there was a little bit of distance sometimes, especially when the noise from the jackhammers gets going and the dust is blowing everywhere and you've got drivers honking because they think their time is more important than the safety of the crew. But no, everything is always very sharp, very present, very real. \n\nI've never had any weird visual things, either. Like, the stop sign always looks exactly like a stop sign. The cars always look like cars. The trees don't look weird, the sky doesn't look weird. Everything is exactly where it's supposed to be, exactly how it's supposed to look. It's just my brain that sort of... unplugs from it all. But the world itself? And my connection to my own body? That all stays perfectly normal, perfectly solid. It's just the thoughts that vanish. So, yeah, none of that other stuff you mentioned. Just the blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of memory loss or feeling disconnected from your thoughts or surroundings outside of these blank spells while you're working?\n\nPatient: No, no memory loss. And I don't feel disconnected outside of work either. I mean, my memory is actually pretty sharp, all things considered. I can remember the license plate of that guy in the silver Honda I mentioned earlier, for example—it's one of those custom state park plates, the ones with the little blue heron on it, and it ends in 4-G-T. I remember that because I always think, \"Four Grand Touring,\" like the car, even though it's a Honda Civic. So my memory is fine. \n\nAnd as for feeling disconnected when I'm not on the job... no. When I'm at home, I'm just at home. I mean, I'm tired, obviously. You work a ten or twelve-hour shift on your feet, holding a sign that catches the wind like a sail, you're going to be exhausted. I get home, I take off those boots—the ones I really need the gel inserts for—and I just kind of collapse on the couch. But I know I'm on the couch. I know I'm watching Jeopardy, or whatever happens to be on. I know I need to get up and put a frozen pizza in the oven, even if I don't have the energy to actually do it. \n\nI'm very aware of my surroundings, my apartment, the sound of the neighbor upstairs who apparently bowls with medicine balls every Tuesday night. I don't lose time. I don't suddenly \"wake up\" and wonder how I got to the kitchen. I don't forget conversations I've had with my sister when she calls to check in, even if I don't really feel like talking to her because I'm so drained. I remember everything we talked about—how her kids are doing in soccer, how her husband's promotion is going. \n\nIt's really, truly just those specific moments out there on the asphalt when the thoughts just... stop. It's not a memory thing, because once the spell passes, I know exactly what just happened. I know I just stood there like a statue for two minutes. I remember the panic of it. I remember the cars waiting. I just don't have any thoughts *during* it. But outside of that? No, my memory is completely intact, and I'm always fully connected to whatever boring, exhausting thing I happen to be doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or like you were watching life through a foggy window, even when you weren’t having one of these blank episodes at work?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, it's never like that. A foggy window... I mean, honestly, sometimes I wish I had a foggy window to look through, just to soften things up a bit, you know? Give my eyes a break. But no. \n\nEven when I'm not on the site, even when I'm just on my days off or running errands... everything is crystal clear. I'll give you an example. I go to the grocery store, right? The big one down on Elm Street, where the fluorescent lights are so bright they practically hum. I'm walking down the cereal aisle, and there is absolutely no fog. I see every single brightly colored box, I see the price tags—which, by the way, are absolutely ridiculous right now. I mean, almost seven dollars for a box of generic bran flakes? When I was getting my bachelor's degree—which feels like a lifetime ago, honestly, and believe me, I spend a lot of time wondering how I ended up standing on asphalt holding a stop sign after four years of university—but back then, you could feed yourself for a week on seven dollars. \n\nBut anyway, my point is, I'm standing there in the aisle, and I can hear the squeak of a shopping cart wheel from the lady three aisles over. I can see the little smudges on the glass of the freezer doors where somebody's toddler pressed their sticky hands. I can feel the cold air blasting out of the dairy section right onto my ankles. It's all just... aggressively real. \n\nThere's no detachment at all. There's no feeling like it's a movie or a dream or like I'm separated from it by glass. I'm just a forty-one-year-old woman standing in a freezing grocery store staring at overpriced bran flakes, feeling the ache in my lower back from standing on the road all week. It's mundane, it's exhausting, and it is one hundred percent real life. I never get that disconnected feeling you're talking about, not at work, and definitely not at home or anywhere else. It's just those blank spells I told you about. That is literally the only thing. The rest of the time, the window is perfectly, annoyingly clean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you suddenly felt dazed, confused, or found yourself doing things without being fully aware of it, like wandering somewhere or performing routine actions without remembering why?\n\nPatient: No, never. I've never wandered off or found myself somewhere without knowing how I got there. Like I was saying about my memory being sharp, I always know exactly what I'm doing and why I'm doing it. Even when I'm doing things on autopilot, you know? \n\nLike, take my morning routine. I get up at 4:30 AM, which is ungodly, but you have to be at the site early before the rush hour traffic starts getting really aggressive. So I get up, I make my coffee—just instant, I don't have the patience or the energy for a French press or anything fancy at that hour, not that I could afford the good beans anyway. I put on my high-vis vest, I lace up those boots with the gel inserts, I grab my thermos, and I drive to the site. It's the same routine every single day, five days a week, sometimes six if they need me for a weekend closure. I could do it in my sleep. \n\nBut I'm not *actually* asleep when I do it. I'm fully aware. I know I'm making the instant coffee. I know I'm lacing up the boots. I don't ever snap out of a daze and think, \"Wait, how did I get my boots on?\" or \"Why am I holding my car keys?\" I know exactly why I'm holding them. Because if I don't go to work, I don't get paid, and if I don't get paid, I can't pay the rent on my apartment, which they just raised by another hundred dollars a month, by the way. \n\nSo, no, there's no wandering, no confusion, no doing things without realizing it. Even when I'm exhausted, even when my brain feels like it's been put through a blender from the noise and the heat and the stress of trying to keep people from driving their cars into a ditch, I am always aware of what I'm doing. It's just those blank spells, right there on the job, where everything just stops. But I'm never doing anything during those spells. I'm just standing there. I'm not wandering into traffic or twirling my sign around without knowing it. I'm just frozen. So, no, none of that confused wandering stuff. Not at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, never. I mean, honestly, sometimes I think it would be a relief to forget who I am for a few minutes, just to take a vacation from my own life, you know? Give myself a break from the reality of it all. But no, I always know exactly who I am. I'm a forty-one-year-old woman who spent four years in college getting a degree just to end up wearing neon yellow and holding a piece of reflective metal for twelve hours a day. I never forget that. It's etched into my brain. 

I know my name, I know I live in apartment 4B with the leaky kitchen faucet that the landlord refuses to fix—I've called him three times this month, by the way, and he just keeps saying he'll "get to it," which we all know means he's hoping I'll just give up and buy a wrench and fix it myself. I know I'm standing on the corner of Route 9 and Miller Road when I'm at work. I never suddenly look around and think, "Who am I? What city is this? How did I get here?" 

It's actually the exact opposite. I am painfully aware of who I am and every single choice that led me to this exact point in my life. Every time I'm standing there, feeling the heat radiate up from the asphalt through the soles of my boots—even with those gel inserts, which really only do so much after the eighth hour, let me tell you—I am fully aware of my identity. I know my social security number, I know my sister's phone number by heart, I know exactly what I'm doing and why I have to do it. 

So no, no amnesia, no forgetting my own name or anything dramatic like you see in the movies. Like I've been trying to explain, it's really just those specific moments on the job where my brain just hits a brick wall and the thoughts completely evaporate. But even then, I don't forget *me*. The thoughts just stop. But my identity, my memory of where I am and what my life is... that never goes away. It's always right there, heavy as lead, every single day.
